    IP addresses

    An IP address is a logical identifier assigned to a device when it connects to a network.
    IP ,smskhla hkq cd,hlg iïnkaO jk úg Wmdx.hlg mjrk ;d¾lsl y÷kd.ekSuls'

    It identifies the device at a higher level, such as within a larger network or the internet.
    th úYd, cd,hla fyda wka;¾cd,h jeks by< uÜgul§ Wmdx.h y÷kd .kS'

    Unlike MAC addresses, which are tied to hardware, IP addresses are logical and can change.
    oDVdx. iuÕ ne£ we;s MAC ,smsk fuka fkdj" IP ,smsk ;d¾lsl jk w;r fjkia úh yel'

    For instance, a device’s IP address may change if it connects to a different network.
    WodyrKhla f,i" Wmdx.hla fjkia cd,hlg iïnkaO jqjfyd;a tys IP ,smskh fjkia úh yel'

    Correct Answer / ksjerÈ ms<s;=r"

    The given IP address is
    ,nd § we;s IP ,smskh

    ,smskhhs

    /24 means the first 24 bits are for the network part, and the remaining 8 bits are for hosts
    /24 hkafkka woyia lrkafka m<uq ìgq 24 cd, fldgi i|yd jk w;r b;sß ìgq 8 Odrl i|yd fõ

    Wmcd,h 172.16.48.0/24 fõ" w¾:h

    Network address: 172.16.48.0

    Valid host addresses: from 172.16.48.1 to 172.16.48.254
    j,x.= Odrl ,smsk( 172.16.48.1 isg 172.16.48.254

 6. In TCP/IP computer networks, Transport Protocol Data Unit (TPDU) is referred to as a
    TCP/IP mß.Kl cd,h ;=< Transport Protocol Data Unit (TPDU) f,i i|yka jkqfha"

    1) packet             2) frame                3) segment           4) window              5) message
       melÜgqjlah'           rduqjlah'               fldgilah'            ljq¿jlah'              mKsúvhlah'

    Answer: 3)

    A TCP/IP computer network is a communication network that uses the TCP/IP protocol suite to connect
    computers and devices so they can send and receive data over the Internet or any other digital network.
    TCP/IP mß.Kl cd,hla hkq mß.Kl iy Wmdx. iïnkaO lsÍu i|yd TCP/IP fm%dfgdaflda, lÜg,h
    Ndú;d lrk ikaksfõok cd,hla jk w;r tuÕska wka;¾cd,h fyda fjk;a ´kEu äðg,a cd,hla
    yryd o;a; heùug iy ,nd .ekSug yelsh'

    The Four Layers of TCP/IP Model / TCP/IP wdlD;sfha ia:r y;r

             Layer                               Function                         Example Protocols
      Application Layer         User services like web, email, file transfer    HTTP, FTP, SMTP, DNS
        Transport Layer                End-to-end communication                       TCP, UDP
         Internet Layer                  Routing of data packets                      IP, ICMP
     Network Access Layer                 Physical transmission                    Ethernet, Wi-Fi
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                -73-                                           #ictfromabc



    A Transport Protocol Data Unit (TPDU) is the data unit used at the transport layer in a computer network.
    It is the formatted block of data that the transport layer sends to network layer for delivery across network.
    m%jdyk fm%dfgdaflda, o;a; tAllhla hkq mß.Kl cd,hl m%jdyk ia:rfha Ndú;d jk o;a; tAllhhs'
    th m%jdyk ia:rh cd,hla yryd fnod yeÍu i|yd cd, ia:rhg hjk wdlD;s.; o;a; fldgihs'

    A packet is a formatted unit of data carried by the Network Layer (Layer 3) of the TCP/IP or OSI models.
    It is the Protocol Data Unit at the network layer and plays a key role in delivering data across networks.
    melÜgqjla hkq TCP/IP fyda OSI wdlD;sfha cd, ia:rh ^3 jk ia:rh& u.ska f.k hk o;a;j,
    wdlD;s.; tAllhls' th cd, ia:rfha fm%dfgdaflda, o;a; tAllh (PDU) jk w;r cd, yryd o;a;
    ,nd §fï§ m%Odk ld¾hNdrhla bgq lrhs'

    A frame is the Protocol Data Unit at the Data Link Layer (Layer 2) of the OSI model. It is the container
    used to encapsulate packets before data is transmitted over the physical medium (like cables or wireless).
    rduqjla hkq OSI wdlD;sfha o;a; ine¢ ia:rfha ^2 jk ia:rh& fm%dfgdaflda, o;a; tAllh (PDU) fõ'
    th fN!;sl udOHh yryd ^flan,a fyda /yeka rys; jeks& o;a; iïfm%aIKh lsÍug fmr melÜ
    lemaishq, lsÍug Ndú;d lrk nyd¨u fõ'

    A segment is the Protocol Data Unit (PDU) at the Transport Layer (Layer 4) of the OSI model, specifically
    when using the TCP protocol. / úfYaIfhka TCP fm%dfgdaflda,h Ndú;d lrk úg" OSI wdlD;sfha m%jdyk
    ia:rfha ^4 jk ia:rh& fm%dfgdaflda, o;a; tAllh (PDU) fldgila fõ'

    Correct Answer / ksjerÈ ms<s;=r"
    3) segment / fldgi

    In the TCP/IP model, each layer encapsulates data with its own header, and the resulting unit has a specific
    name. / TCP/IP wdlD;sfha" iEu ia:rhlau ;ukaf.au YS¾Ihla iuÕ o;a; lemaishqf,aÜ lrk w;r"
    m%;sM,hla f,i tAllhg ksYaÑ; kula we;'

     Application Layer
                                  Message
        fhÿï ia:r
      Transport Layer
                       Segment (TCP) / Datagram (UDP)
       m%jdyk ia:r
      Network Layer
                                   Packet
         cd, ia:r
     Data Link Layer
                                   Frame
     o;a; ine¢ ia:r
      Physical Layer
                                    Bits
       fN!;sl ia:r

    The Transport Layer is responsible for end-to-end communication, error checking, and retransmission. In
    TCP (Transmission Control Protocol), the Transport Protocol Data Unit (TPDU) is called a Segment.
    m%jdyk ia:rh wka;fha isg wjidkh olajd ikaksfõokh" fodaI mÍlaId lsÍu iy kej; iïfm%aIKh
    i|yd j.lsj hq;=h' TCP ^iïfm%aIK md,k fm%dfgdaflda,h& ys" m%jdyk fm%dfgdaflda, o;a; tAllh (TPDU)
    fldgi f,i ye¢kafõ'

    Therefore, option 3) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 3) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 4. Consider the following statements.
    my; m%ldY i,lkak'

     A. TCP is a connection oriented and a reliable protocol.
        TCP hkq iïnkaO;d keUqre (connection oriented) úYajdi iy.; kshudj,shls (protocol).
     B. UDP is a connectionless and an unreliable protocol.
        TCP and UDP are transport layer protocols.
     C. UDP hkq iïnkaO;d rys; (connectionless) úYajdifhka f;dr kshudj,shls'

     1) A only                             2) B only                      3) A and B only
        A muKs                                B muKs                         A iy B muKs
     4) B and C only                       5) All A, B and C
        B iy C muKs                           A, B iy C ish,a,u

   reliability through features like acknowledgments, retransmissions, and error-checking. / ksjerÈhs' TCP

 5. In the OSI reference model, the network layer is responsible for ............................. communication.
    OSI iuqoafoaY wdlD;sfhys cd, ia:rh .............................. ikaksfõokh i|yd odhl fõ'
     Which of the following is suitable to fill the blank in the above statement?
     by; m%ldYfhys ysia;ek msrùug my; ljrla fhda.H fõ o?
     1)   node to node / mqrelfha isg mqrelhg (node to node)
     2)   source to destination / m%Njfha isg .ukdka;hg
     3)   hop to hop / msïfuka msïug
     4)   switch to router / iaúpfhys isg ud¾.ldrlhg
     5)   process to process / l%shdj,sfhka l%shdj,shg

                                                                                                     ( 2018 - 05 )
     Answer: 2)
     The network layer (Layer 3) in the OSI model is responsible for logical addressing, routing, and
     forwarding packets from the source host to the destination host across multiple networks.
     OSI wdlD;sfha cd, ia:rh (3 jk ia:rh) ;d¾lsl wduka;%Kh" ud¾..; lsÍu iy nyq cd, yryd
     m%Nj Odrlfha isg .ukdka; Odrlhg melÜ fhduq lsÍu i|yd j.lsj hq;=h'

     It ensures end-to-end (source-to-destination) communication by determining the best path for data
     transmission, handling IP addressing, and managing congestion control.
     th o;a; iïfm%aIKhg fyd|u ud¾.h ;SrKh lsÍu"IP ,smsk yeisrùu iy ;onoh md,kh l<ukdlr-
     -Kh lsÍu u.ska wka;fha isg wjidkh olajd ^m%Njfhka .ukdka;hg& ikaksfõokh iy;sl lrhs'

     Why the Other Options Are Incorrect / fjk;a úl,am jerÈ jkafka wehs

     1) node to node mqrelfha isg mqrelhg
        Incorrect: This describes data link layer (Layer 2) functionality, which ensures communication
        between directly connected nodes (e.g., switches, routers, or devices on the same network segment).
        jerÈhs( fuh o;a; ine¢ ia:rh (2 jk ia:rh) l%shdldß;ajh úia;r lrhs" th iDcqju iïnkaê;
        node (Wod( iaúp" rjqgr fyda tlu cd, fldgfia Wmdx.) w;r ikaksfõokh iy;sl lrhs'

          The network layer operates at a higher level, managing communication across multiple hops
          (networks), not just between adjacent nodes.
          cd, ia:rh by< uÜgul l%shd;aul jk w;r" hdno node w;r muKla fkdj nyq hops (cd,)
          yryd ikaksfõokh l<ukdlrKh lrhs'

     3) hop to hop msïfuka msïug
        Incorrect: Similar to "node to node," this refers to data link layer (Layer 2) or physical layer (Layer 1)
        operations, where data moves between intermediate devices (e.g., routers, switches).
        jerÈhs( "mqrelfha isg mqrelhg" g iudk" fuh o;a; ine¢ ia:rh (2 jk ia:rh) fyda fN!;sl
        ia:rh (1 jk ia:rh) fufyhqï j,g fhduq lrhs" tys§ o;a; w;rueÈ Wmdx. (Wod( rjqgr" iaúp)
        w;r p,kh fõ'

          The network layer focuses on the entire path from source to destination, not individual hops.
          cd, ia:rh wjOdkh fhduq lrkafka uQ,dY%fha isg .ukdka;h olajd we;s iïmQ¾K ud¾.h flfrys
          ñi ;ks fydma j,g fkdfõ'

     4) switch to router iaúpfhys isg ud¾.ldrlhg
          Incorrect: This is too specific and misleading.
          jerÈhs( fuh b;d ksYaÑ; iy fkduÕ hjk iq¿h'

          Switches operate at Layer 2 (data link layer), forwarding frames based on MAC addresses.
          iaúp 2 jk ia:rfha (o;a; ine¢ ia:rfha)" MAC ,smsk u; mokïj rduq fhduq lsÍfï§ l%shd;aulfõ'

          Routers operate at Layer 3 (network layer), forwarding packets based on IP addresses.
          ud¾.ldrl 3 jk ia:rfha (cd, ia:rfha) l%shd;aul fõ" IP ,smsk u; mokïj melÜ fhduq lrhs'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -80-                                          #ictfromabc



          The network layer's role is broader than just switch-router communication. it handles any source-to-
          destination routing, including hosts, servers, and networks.
          cd, ia:rfha ld¾hNdrh iaúÉ-rjqg¾ ikaksfõokhg jvd mq¿,a h. th Odrl" fiajdodhlhka iy
          cd, we;=¿j ´kEu uQ,dY%fhka .ukdka;hg ud¾..; lsÍu yiqrejhs'

    5) prcoess to process l%shdj,sfhka l%shdj,shg
       Incorrect: This describes transport layer (Layer 4) functionality, where protocols like TCP/UDP ensure
       communication between applications or processes (e.g., web browser to web server).
       jerÈhs( fuh m%jdyk ia:rfha (4 jk ia:rh) l%shdldß;ajh úia;r lrhs" tys§ TCP/UDP jeks
       kshudj,s fhÿï fyda l%shdj,Ska w;r ikaksfõokh iy;sl lrhs (Wod( fjí w;sßlaiqfõ isg fjí
       fiajdodhlh olajd)'

          The network layer does not manage process-level communication. it deals with host-to-host packet
          delivery. / cd, ia:rh l%shdj,s uÜgfï ikaksfõokh l<ukdlrKh fkdlrhs' th host-to-host
          melÜ fnod yeÍu iuÕ lghq;= lrhs'

    Therefore, the correct answer is 2
    tneúka" ksjerÈ ms<s;=r 2 fõ.

 6. Which of the following indicates the number of host bits and the number of IP addresses respectively
    in a class C network? / my; ljrla C mka;sfha cd,hl we;s i;aldrl (host) ìgq .Kk iy IP
    ,smsk .Kk ms<sfj<ska olajhs o?

    1) 8 and 256                      2) 8 and 65536                  3) 16 and 256
    4) 16 and 65536                   5) 24 and 256

                                                                                                  ( 2018 - 08 )
    Answer: 1)

    In classful IP addressing, IPv4 addresses are divided into Class A, B and C networks based on their first
    few bits. / mka;suh IP ,smsklrKfha§" IPv4 ,smsk tAjdfha m<uq ìgq lsysmh u; mokïj mka;s A, B
    iy C cd, j,g fnod we;.

      Class First       Default Network IP Range                   Network      Host      Usable Hosts
            Bits        Mask                                       Bits         Bits      per Network
      A     0           255.0.0.0 (/8)    1.0.0.1 –                8 bits       24        16,777,214 (2²⁴ -
                                          126.255.255.254                       bits      2)
      B        10       255.255.0.0 (/16) 128.0.0.1 –              16 bits      16        65,534 (2¹⁶ - 2)
                                          191.255.255.254                       bits
      C        110      255.255.255.0     192.0.0.1 –              24 bits      8 bits    254 (2⁸ - 2)
                        (/24)             223.255.255.254

    Key Characteristics of Class C Networks / C mka;sfha cd, j, m%Odk ,laIK

    Starts with 192 to 223 in the first octet.
    m<uq wIagulfha 192 isg 223 olajd wdrïN fõ'
    Default subnet mask: 255.255.255.0 (or /24 in CIDR notation).
    fmrksñ Wmcd, wdjrKh( 255.255.255.0 (fyda $24 CIDR wxlkfha)

    Network portion: First 24 bits (3 octets)
    cd, fldgi( m<uq ìgq 24 (wIagul 3)

    Host portion: Last 8 bits (1 octet)
    i;aldrl fldgi( wjidk ìgq 8 (wIagul 1)
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                             -81-                                     #ictfromabc



    Host Bits in Class C / C mka;sfha i;aldrl ìgq
    Since Class C reserves 24 bits for the network, the remaining 8 bits are for hosts.
    C mka;sh cd,h i|yd ìgq 24la fjka lr we;s neúka" b;sß ìgq 8 i;aldrl i|yd fõ'

    Host bits = 8 (last octet).
    i;aldrl ìgq } 8 ^wka; wIagul&'

    Total IP Addresses / uq¿ IP ,smsk
    With 8 host bits, the total possible addresses = 28 = 256
    i;aldrl ìgq 8la iuÕska" úh yels uq¿ ,smsk = 28 = 256

    However, 2 addresses are reserved / flfia fj;;a" ,smsk 2la fjka lr we;

    Network ID / cd, ye÷kqïm; (e.g., 192.168.1.0)
    Broadcast IP / úldYk IP (e.g., 192.168.1.255)
    Usable hosts / Ndú;d l< yels i;aldrl = 254 (256 - 2)

    The question asks for total IP addresses (256), not usable hosts, so 256 is correct.
    m%Yakh Ndú;d l< yels Odrl fkdj uq¿ IP ,smsk (256) i|yd wihs" tneúka 256 ksjerÈ fõ'

    Therefore, the correct answer is 1.
    tneúka" ksjerÈ ms<s;=r 1 fõ'

 7. To which of the following network classes does the IP address 192.248.254.1 belong?
    my; oelafjk l=uk cd, mka;shlg 192.248.254.1 hk IP ,smskh wh;a fõ o?

    1) A            2) B             3) C          4) D           5) E

    Answer: 3)

    192.248.254.1 is a class C IP address. As explained in the above question, a class C IP address has;
    192.248.254.1 hkq C mka;sfha IP ,smskhls' by; m%Yakfha meyeÈ,s lr we;s mßÈ" C mka;sfha IP
    ,smskhlg we;af;a;

    Starts with 192 to 223 in the first octet.
    m<uq wIagulfha 192 isg 223 olajd wdrïN fõ'

    Default subnet mask: 255.255.255.0 (or /24 in CIDR notation).
    fmrksñ Wmcd, wdjrKh( 255.255.255.0 (or CIDR wxlkh /24) wxlkfha $24&'

    Network portion: First 24 bits (3 octets).
    cd, fldgi( m<uq ìgq 24 ^wIagul 3&'

    Host portion: Last 8 bits (1 octet).
    i;aldrl fldgi( wjidk ìgq 8 ^wIagul 1&'

    Since this IP address starts with 192, this is a class C IP address.

    Therefore, the correct answer is 3.

 8. In the OSI reference model, a protocol data unit of the network layer is referred to as a ......................
    Which of the following terms is suitable to fill the blank in the above statement?
    OSI iuqoafoaY wdlD;sfhys cd, ia:rfhys kshudj,s o;a; tallhla (protocol data unit )
    ............................. la f,i ye¢kafõ' my; m%ldYfhys ysia;ek msrùu i|yd my; ljrla iqÿiq fõo?

     1) frame / rduqj (frame)          2) segment / LKavh                 3) window / ljq¿j
     4) message / mKsúvh               5) packet / melÜgqj

     Answer: 5)
     In the OSI model, the network layer (Layer 3) is responsible for logical addressing, routing, and forwarding
     data between networks. / OSI wdlD;sfha" cd, ia:rh (3 jk ia:rh) ;d¾lsl ,smsk" ud¾..; lsÍu iy
     cd, w;r o;a; fhduq lsÍu i|yd j.lsj hq;=h'
     The Protocol Data Unit (PDU) at this layer is called a packet.
     fuu ia:rfha we;s kshudj,sh o;a; tAllh (PDU) melÜgqjla f,i ye¢kafõ'
     Packets contain source and destination IP addresses and are encapsulated into frames at the data link layer
     (Layer 2). / melÜ j, uQ,dY% iy .ukdka; IP ,smsk wvx.= jk w;r o;a; ine¢ ia:rfha (2 jk
     ia:rh) rduq j,g we;=<;a lr we;'

     Frame / rduqj
     •   Layer: Data Link Layer (Layer 2)
         ia:rh( o;a; ine¢ ia:rh (2 jk ia:rh)
     •   Frames are the protocol data unit for Layer 2, not the network layer.
         rduq hkq cd, ia:rh fkdj 2 jk ia:rh i|yd kshudj,s o;a; tAllh fõ'
     •   Frames include MAC addresses and error-checking.
         rduq j,g MAC ,smsk iy fodaI mÍlaIdj we;=<;a fõ'

     Segment / LKavh
     •   Layer: Transport Layer (Layer 4).
         ia:rh( m%jdyk ia:rh (4 jk ia:rh)
     •   Segments are the protocol data unit for Layer 4 (e.g., TCP segments or UDP datagrams).
         LKav hkq 4 jk ia:rh i|yd kshudj,s o;a; tAllh fõ (Wod( TCP fldgia fyda UDP o;a;.%Eï)'
     •   They include port numbers for process-to-process communication.
         l%shdj,sfhka l%shdj,shg ikaksfõokh i|yd tAjdg port wxl we;=<;a fõ'

     Window / ljq¿j
     •   Not a protocol data unit in any OSI layer.
         lsisÿ OSI ia:rhl kshudj,s o;a; tAllhla fkdfõ'
     •   "Window" refers to flow control mechanisms (e.g., TCP sliding window), not a data unit.
         "ljq¿j" hkq o;a; tAllhla fkdj m%jdy md,k hdka;%K ^Wod( TCP ia,hsäka ljq¿j& hls'

     Message / mKsúvh
     •   Layer: Application Layer (Layer 7).
         ia:rh( fhÿï ia:rh (7 jk ia:rh)'
     •   Messages are high-level application data (e.g., HTTP requests, emails).
         mKsúv hkq by< uÜgfï fhÿï o;a; (Wod( HTTP b,a,Sï" Bfï,a)'
     •   They are encapsulated into lower-layer protocol data unit (segments, packets, frames).
         tAjd my< ia:r kshudj,s o;a; tAll (fldgia" melÜ" rduq) j,g lemaishq, lr we;'

   1. Use of public key and private key in encryption and decryption processes is called
      fmdÿ h;=r (public key) iy fm!oa.,sl h;=r (private key) Ndú;fhka isÿ flfrk .=ma; fla;kh
      (encryption) iy .=ma; úfla;kh (decryption) y÷kajkq ,nkafka"
          1)    asymmetric encryption / wiuñ;sl .=ma; fla;kh f,i h'
          2)    digital encryption / ixLHdxl .=ma; fla;kh f,i h'
          3)    hybrid encryption / fouqyqï .=ma; fla;kh f,i h'
          4)    private key encryption / fm!oa.,sl h;=re .=ma; fla;kh f,i h'
          5)    symmetric encryption / iuñ;sl .=ma; fla;kh f,i h'
                                                                                                                                                         ( 2019 - 03 )
          Answer: 1)

          Cryptography - .=ma; fla;kh
          Cryptography is the practice of securing information through techniques that prevent unauthorized access
          or alteration. / .=ma; fla;kh hkq wkjir m%fõYh fyda fjkia lsÍu je<elaùu i|yd ;dlaIKsl l%u
          Ndú;fhka f;dr;=re wdrlaId lsÍfï ms<sfj;ls'

          Encryption- fla;kh
          Encryption is a process where data is converted into a coded format to prevent unauthorized access. It is
          typically achieved using algorithms that transform readable information into ciphertext, which can only
          be decoded with a specific key. Used in communication, storage, and cybersecurity, it ensures data
          confidentiality and integrity.
          fla;kh hkq o;a; wkjirfhka m%fõY ùu je<elaùu i|yd fla;kh l< wdldrhlg mßj¾;kh
          lsÍfï l%shdj,shls' th idudkHfhka Ndú;d lrkafka lshúh yels f;dr;=re fla;sl fm, njg mßj¾;kh
          lrk we,af.dß;u Ndú;d lrñks' fuu fla;sl fm, úfYaIfhka ksYaÑ; h;=rla Ndú;d lrñka muKla
          úfla;kh l< yelsh' ikaksfõokh" .nvd lsÍu iy ihsn¾ wdrlaIdj hk lafIa;%j, Ndú;d jk fuu
          l%shdj,sh" o;a; ryiHNdjh iy wLKav;dj ;yjqre lrhs'

          Decryption - úfla;kh
          Decryption is the process of converting encrypted data back into its original, readable form using a specific
          key or algorithm. It ensures that authorized users can access protected information while maintaining
          security and privacy. Commonly used in secure communication and data protection.
          úfla;kh hkq" .=ma; fla;kh lrk ,o o;a; kej; tys uq,a" lshúh yels wdldrhg mßj¾;kh
          lsÍfï l%shdj,shls' fuh isÿ lrkq ,nkafka úfYaIs; h;=rla fyda we,af.dß;ula Ndú;d lrñks' tu.ska
          wjir ,;a mßYS,lhskag wdrlaIs; f;dr;=re fj; m%fõY ùug yels jk w;r" wdrlaIdj iy
          fm!oa.,sl;ajh mj;ajd .ekSu iy;sl lrhs' fuh idudkHfhka wdrlaIs; ikaksfõokh iy o;a;
          wdrlaIdj i|yd Ndú;d fõ'

          Public Key - fmdÿ h;=r
          A public key is a cryptographic key that can be shared openly without compromising security. It is used
          to encode data or verify authenticity, ensuring secure communication and identity validation. It is shared
          openly and works with a private key, which is kept secret. Data encrypted with the public key can only be
          decrypted using the private key.
          fmdÿ h;=rla hkq wdrlaIdjg ydkshla fkdjk mßÈ újD;j fnod.; yels .=ma; f,aLk h;=rls' th
          o;a; .=ma; fla;kh lsÍug fyda i;H;dj ;yjqre lsÍug" wdrlaIs; ikaksfõokh iy wkkH;d
          j,x.=lrKh iy;sl lsÍug Ndú;d lrhs' th újD;j fnod .kakd w;r ryis.;j ;nd we;s fm!oa.,sl
          h;=rla iuÕ l%shd lrhs' fmdÿ h;=r iuÕ .=ma; fla;kh l< o;a; fm!oa.,sl h;=r Ndú;fhka
          muKla úfla;kh l< yelsh'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                -84-                                          #ictfromabc



    Private Key - fm!oa.,sl h;=r
    Private key is a secret cryptographic key used to decrypt data or generate digital signatures. It is securely
    held by the owner and must remain confidential, as it provides access to protected information. Only the
    holder of the private key can unlock or authenticate data intended for them, ensuring security and control.
    fm!oa.,sl h;=r hkq o;a; úfla;kh lsÍug fyda äðg,a w;aika ckkh lsÍug Ndú;d lrk ryia
    .=ma; fla;k h;=rls' th wdrlaIs;j ;nd.kakd w;r" th ryia ;ekam;a l< hq;=h' wdrlaIs; f;dr;=re
    fj; m%fõYh ,ndfok neúka" fm!oa.,sl h;=r ysñhdg muKla tu o;a; w.=¿ yeÍug fyda i;Hdmkh
    lsÍug yelsh' fuu.ska wdrlaIdj iy md,kh iy;sl fõ'

    1) Asymmetric encryption - wiuñ;sl .=ma; fla;kh
       Asymmetric encryption uses a public key to encrypt data and a private key to decrypt it, ensuring
       secure communication.
       wiuñ;sl .=ma; fla;kh hkq o;a; fla;kh lsÍug fmdÿ h;=rla iy th úfla;kh lsÍug
       fm!oa.,sl h;=r Ndú;d lrk wdrlaIs; ikaksfõok l%uhls'
        How It Works / tys l%shdldÍ;ajh
        A pair of keys (public and private) is created using cryptographic algorithms. The sender encrypts the
        data using the recipient’s public key, making it unreadable. The encrypted data is sent over a network
        securely. The recipient uses their private key to decrypt and access the original data. This method,
        encrypts and decrypts data using a public key and a private key.
        .=ma; fla;k we,af.dß;u Ndú;fhka h;=re hq.,hla ^fmdÿ iy fm!oa.,sl& ks¾udKh lr we;'
        hjkakd ,nkakdf.a fmdÿ h;=r Ndú;fhka o;a; fla;kh lrhs" th lshúh fkdyel' fla;kh l,
        o;a; cd,hla yryd wdrlaIs;j hjkq ,efí' ,nkakd uq,a o;a; úfla;kh lsÍug iy m%fõY
        lsÍug Tjqkaf.a fm!oa.,sl h;=r Ndú;d lrhs' fmdÿ h;=r iy fm!oa.,sl h;=rla Ndú;d lrñka
        o;a; fla;kh yd úfla;kh lsÍu isÿ lrkafka fuu l%shdj,sfhaÈh'

    2) Digital encryption - ixLHdxl .=ma; fla;kh
       Digital encryption works by transforming plaintext into ciphertext using cryptographic algorithms and
       a key, making the data unreadable without decryption.
       wxls; fla;kh l%shd lrkafka .=ma;fla;k we,af.dß;u iy h;=rla Ndú;fhka ir, mdG fla;sl
       mdG njg mßj¾;kh lsÍfuks" úfla;khlska f;drj o;a; lshúh fkdyel'
        How It Works / tys l%shdldÍ;ajh
        A unique key or key pair is created for encryption and decryption. The plaintext data is processed
        using an algorithm and the key, converting it into ciphertext. The encrypted data is sent over a network
        or stored securely. The recipient or system uses the correct key to reverse the encryption, restoring the
        original plaintext. In this process, a unique key is used.
        fla;kh iy úfla;kh i|yd wkkH h;=rla fyda h;=re hq.,hla idokq ,efí' ir, mdG o;a;
        we,af.dß;uhla iy h;=r Ndú;fhka ilikq ,nk w;r th fla;sl fm< njg mßj¾;kh lrhs'
        fla;kh l, o;a; cd,hla yryd hjkq ,efí fyda wdrlaIs;j .nvd lr we;' ,nkakd fyda moaO;sh
        fla;kh wdmiq yerùug ksjerÈ h;=r Ndú;d lrhs" uq,a ir, fm< m%;sia:dmkh lrhs' fuu
        l%shdj,sfha Ndú; jkafka wkkH h;=rls'

    3) Hybrid encryption - fouqyqï .=ma; fla;kh
       Hybrid encryption is a method that combines the security of asymmetric encryption with the efficiency
       of symmetric encryption. It is used to achieve both speed and strong data protection in secure
       communication systems. / fouqyqï .=ma; fla;kh hkq wiuñ;sl .=ma; fla;khl wdrlaIdj iy
       iuñ;sl .=ma; fla;dxlkfha ld¾hlaIu;djh tAldnoaO lrk l%uhls' wdrlaIs; ikaksfõok moaO;sj,
       fõ.h iy Yla;su;a o;a; wdrlaIdj hk folu ,nd .ekSug th Ndú;d lrhs'
        How It Works / tys l%shdldÍ;ajh
        A random symmetric key is generated for encrypting the actual data. The plaintext data is encrypted
        using the symmetric key, making encryption faster. The symmetric key itself is encrypted using
        asymmetric encryption (public key). The encrypted data and the encrypted symmetric key are sent to
        the recipient. The recipient uses their private key to decrypt the symmetric key, then uses it to decrypt
        the actual data. / i;H o;a; fla;kh lsÍu i|yd wyUq iuñ;sl h;=rla ckkh flfra' ir, fm<
        o;a; iuñ;sl h;=r Ndú;fhka fla;kh lr" fla;kh fõ.j;a lrhs' iuñ;sl h;=r" wiuñ;sl
        fla;kh ^fmdÿ h;=r& Ndú;fhka fla;kh lr we;' fla;kh l, o;a; iy fla;kh l, iuñ;sl
        h;=r ,nkakd fj; hjkq ,efí' ,nkakd iuñ;sl h;=r úfla;kh lsÍug Tjqkaf.a mqoa.,sl h;=r
        Ndú;d lrhs" miqj ienE o;a; úfla;kh lsÍug th Ndú;d lrhs'

    Symmetric encryption - iuñ;sl .=ma; fla;kh
    Symmetric encryption relies on a single secret key that is shared between the sender and recipient. It does
    not use public or private keys.
    iuñ;sl .=ma; fla;kh hjkakd iy ,nkakd w;r fnod .kakd ;ks ryia h;=rla u; r|d mj;S' th
    fmdÿ fyda fm!oa.,sl h;=re Ndú;d fkdlrhs'

    Hybrid encryption uses a unique symmetric key to encrypt the actual data and a public key to encrypt that
    symmetric key. The private key is used to decrypt the symmetric key.
    fouqyqï .=ma; fla;kh ienE o;a; fla;kh lsÍug wkkH iuñ;sl h;=rla iy tu iuñ;sl h;=r
    fla;kh lsÍug fmdÿ h;=rla Ndú;d lrhs' fm!oa.,sl h;=r iuñ;sl h;=r úfla;kh lsÍug Ndú;d

    4) Private key encryption / fm!oa.,sl h;=re .=ma; fla;kh
       A private key is a confidential cryptographic key used to decrypt data or create digital signatures. It is
       uniquely paired with a public key and must be securely stored, as it provides access to sensitive
       information. Only the holder of the private key can unlock or authenticate data intended for them,
       ensuring security and control. This only uses a private key.
       fm!oa.,sl h;=rla hkq o;a; úfla;kh lsÍug fyda äðg,a w;aika iE§ug Ndú;d lrk ryis.;
       .=ma;fla;k h;=rls' th fmdÿ h;=rla iuÕ wkkH f,i hq., lr we;s w;r th ixfõ§ f;dr;=re

    5) Symmetric encryption / iuñ;sl .=ma; fla;kh
       Symmetric encryption is a cryptographic method where the same key is used for both encrypting and
       decrypting data. This key must be shared securely between the sender and receiver.
       iuñ;sl .=ma; fla;kh hkq o;a; fla;kh lsÍu iy ixfla;kh lsÍu hk folgu tlu h;=r
       Ndú;d lrk .=ma; f,aLk l%uhls' fuu h;=r hjkakd iy ,nkakd w;r wdrlaIs;j fnod.; hq;=h'

        How It Works / tys l%shdldÍ;ajh
        The sender uses the shared key to convert plaintext (readable data) into ciphertext (unreadable data).
        The encrypted data is sent to the receiver. The receiver uses the same key to convert the ciphertext
        back into plaintext.
        hjkakd ir, fm< ^lshúh yels o;a;& fla;sl ^lshúh fkdyels o;a;& njg mßj¾;kh lsÍug
        fnod.;a h;=r Ndú; lrhs' fla;kh l< o;a; .%dylhd fj; hjkq ,efí' .%dylhd fla;sl fm<
        kej; ir, fm< njg mßj¾;kh lsÍug tu h;=ru Ndú;d lrhs'
    According to this explanation answer should be 1 )

 2. In a particular network, each node is connected directly to a central network device. This topology
    is referred to as a
    tla;rd mß.Kl cd,hl iEu fkdavqjlau (node) uOH.; cd, Wml%uhlg (central network device)
    Rcqju iïnkaO lr we;' fujeks ia:,lhla (topology) y÷kajkq ,nkafkaa"

    1) bus / nia ia:,lhla f,i h'                           2) hybrid / fouqyqï ia:,lhla f,i h'
    3) mesh / oe,a ia:,lhla f,i h'                         4) ring / uqÿ ia:,lhla f,i h'
    5) star /;drld ia:,lhla f,i h'

    Answer: 5)

    Network Topology / cd, ia:, úoHdj
    Network topology refers to the arrangement of devices, connections, and communication paths in a
    network. It defines how data flows between nodes and impacts performance, scalability, and reliability.
    cd, ia:, úoHdj hkq cd,hl Wmdx." iïnkaO;d iy ikaksfõok ud¾. ieliSuhs' th fkdaâ w;r
    o;a; .,d hk wdldrh iy ld¾h idOkh" mßudKh iy úYajikSh;ajh flfrys n,mdk wdldrh
    ks¾jpkh lrhs'
    Nodes / fkdavq
    A node is a fundamental unit in a network, representing any device or point that can send, receive, or
    process data. Nodes are interconnected to facilitate communication and resource sharing within a network.
    Examples of nodes include Computers, Network Devices, Peripheral Devices, Mobile Devices.
    fkdavqjla hkq o;a; heùug" ,eîug fyda ieliSug yels ´kEu Wmdx.hla fyda ,laIHhla ksfhdackh
    lrk cd,hl uQ,sl tAllhls' cd,hla ;=< ikaksfõokh iy iïm;a fnod.ekSu myiq lsÍu i|yd fkdavq
    tlsfkl iïnkaO fõ' fkdavq j,g WodyrK f,i mß.Kl" cd, Wmdx." mßê Wmdx. iy cx.u Wmdx.
    we;=<;a fõ'

    Central Network Devices / uOHu cd, Wmdx.
    Central network device is a hardware component that manages and controls data traffic between connected
    devices in a network. It ensures efficient communication, security, and resource sharing.
    uOHu cd, Wmdx.h hkq cd,hl iïnkaê; Wmdx. w;r o;a; .ukd.ukh l<ukdlrKh lrk iy
    md,kh lrk oDVdx. ixrplhls' th ld¾hlaIu ikaksfõokh" wdrlaIdj iy iïm;a fnod.ekSu
    iy;sl lrhs'

    •   Hub / kdNsh – Broadcasts data to all connected devices without filtering.
        fmÍulska f;drj iïnkaê; ish¨u Wmdx. fj; o;a; úldYkh lrhs'
    •   Switch / iaúph – Directs data only to the intended recipient, improving efficiency.
        ld¾hlaIu;dj jeäÈhqKq lrñka wfmalaIs; ,nkakdg muKla o;a; fhduq lrhs'
    •   Router / ud¾.lh – Connects different networks and manages data traffic between them.
        úúO cd, iïnkaO lr tAjd w;r o;a; .ukd.ukh l<ukdlrKh lrhs'
    1) Bus Topology / nia ia:,lh
       Bus topology is a network layout where all devices are connected to a single central cable, called the
       bus or backbone. Data is transmitted along this cable, and each device receives the data intended for
       it. When a device sends data, it travels through the bus, and all connected devices receive the signal.
       However, only the device with the matching address processes the data, while others ignore it. Each
       node is not directly connected to a central network device. Instead, all nodes are connected to a single
       communication line.
       nia ia:,lh hkq ish¨u Wmdx. tla uOHu flan,hlg iïnkaO lr we;s cd, ieleiauls" th nih

    2) Hybrid Topology / fouqyqka ia:,lh
       Hybrid topology combines two or more topologies (e.g., star, bus, ring) into a single network. It works
       by creating smaller subnetworks, each with its own topology, and connecting them through a central
       backbone or another topology. Data flows within and between subnetworks. In a hybrid topology,
       whether each node is directly connected to a central network device depends on the specific topologies
       combined. / fouqyqka ia:,lh" ia:,l folla fyda jeä .Kkla ^Wod( ;rej" nih" uqoao& ;ks
       cd,hlg tAldnoaO lrhs' th l%shd lrkafka l=vd Wmcd, ks¾udKh lsÍfuks" tA iEu tllau ;ukaf.au

       iDcqju iïnkaO ù ;sfío hkak r|d mj;skafka tAldnoaO úfYaIs; ia:dk u; h'

        flakaøia:dkhla fyda iaúphla jeks uOHu Wmdx.hlg iïnkaO fõ'

        Wmdx.hla Ndú;d fkdl< yelsh'
    3) Mesh Topology / oe,siuh ia:,lh
       In a mesh topology, each node is not directly connected to a central network device. Instead, every
       node is interconnected with every other node in the network, creating multiple direct paths for data
       transmission. It works by allowing data to travel through various routes, ensuring redundancy and
       reliability. If one path fails, data can be rerouted through another, maintaining uninterrupted
       communication.
       oe,siuh ia:,lfha" iEu fkdavhlau uOHu cd, Wmdx.hlg iDcqju iïnkaO fkdfõ' tA fjkqjg"
       iEu fkdavqjlau cd,fha wfkl=;a iEu fkdavqjla iuÕu wka;¾ iïnkaê; jk w;r" o;a; iïfm%aYkh
       i|yd nyq iDcq ud¾. ks¾udKh lrhs' th l%shd lrkafka o;a; úúO ud¾. yryd .uka lsÍug
       bv §u" w;sßla;h iy úYajikSh;ajh iy;sl lsÍfuks' tla ud¾.hla wid¾:l jqjfyd;a" ndOdjlska
       f;drj ikaksfõokh mj;ajd .ksñka ;j;a ud¾.hla yryd o;a; kej; yrjd heúh yel'

    4) Ring Topology / uqÿ ia:,lh
       Ring topology is a network structure where each node is connected to exactly two other nodes, forming
       a closed loop. Data travels in one or both directions around the ring until it reaches its destination.
       Since there is no central network device, each node acts as a repeater, boosting the signal to ensure
       data reaches the next node. While efficient for small networks, a single node failure can disrupt the
       entire system unless a dual-ring setup is used for redundancy.
       uqÿ ia:,lh hkq cd, jHqyhla jk w;r tys§ iEu fkdavqju ksjerÈj fjk;a fkdavq follg
       iïnkaO lr ixjD; Æmhla idohs' o;a; tys .ukdka;hg <Õd jk f;la j<,a, jgd tla ÈYdjlg
       fyda follska .uka lrhs' uOHu cd, Wmdx.hla fkdue;s neúka" iEu fkdavqjlau mqkrdj¾;lhla
       f,i l%shd lrhs" o;a; B<Õ fkdavqjg <Õd jk nj iy;sl lsÍu i|yd ix{dj jeä lrhs' l=vd
       cd, i|yd ld¾hlaIu jk w;r" w;sßla;h i|yd oaú;aj j<¨ ieliqu Ndú;d fkdl<fyd;a ;ks
       fkdavhla wiu;a ùu iuia; moaO;shu ì| jeáh yelsh'

    5) Star Topology / ;re ia:,lh
       Star topology is a network layout where each node is directly connected to a central network device,
       such as a hub or switch. It works by sending all data through the central device, which then routes it
       to the intended node. This setup simplifies management and troubleshooting, as issues in one node do
       not affect others.
       ;re ia:,lh hkq iEu fkdavqjlau kdNshla fyda iaúphla jeks uOHu cd, Wmdx.hlg iDcqju
       iïnkaO jk cd, ieleiauls' th uOHu Wmdx.h yryd ish¨u o;a; heùfuka l%shd lrhs" th
       wfmalaIs; fkdavqj fj; o;a; fhduq lrhs' fuu ieliqu l<ukdlrKh iy fodaY ksrdlrKh ir,
       lrhs" tla fkdavqjl .eg¿ wfkla tAjdg n,mdkafka ke;'

 3. Which of the following are the properties of a signal?
    my; ljrla ix{djl .=K jkafka o?

    Signal / ix{dj
    A signal is a physical quantity that varies with time, space, or another independent variable, used to
    transmit information. It can be represented in various forms, such as electrical, optical, or sound waves,
    and is categorized as either analog (continuous) or digital (discrete). Signals are essential in
    communication systems, where they are modulated to carry data over distances.
    ix{djla hkq f;dr;=re iïfm%aIKh lsÍug Ndú;d lrk ld,h" wjldYh fyda fjk;a iajdëk úp,Hhla
    wkqj fjkia jk fN!;sl m%udKhls' th úoHq;a" oDYH fyda Yío ;rx. jeks úúO iajrEmj,ska ksrEmKh
    l< yels w;r m%;siu ^wLKav& fyda äðg,a ^úúla;& f,i j¾.SlrKh lr we;' ikaksfõok moaO;sj,
    ix{d w;HjYH jk w;r" tAjd ÿrlg o;a; /f.k hdug uqyq¾ckh lr we;'
    Amplitude / úia;drh
    Amplitude refers to the maximum value a signal reaches in either the positive or negative direction. It
    determines the signal's strength or intensity and is typically measured from the equilibrium position to the
    peak. In electrical signals, it is often expressed in volts, while in sound waves, it is measured in decibels
    or pressure units. / úia;drh hkq ix{djla Ok fyda RK ÈYdjg <Õd jk Wmßu w.hhs' th ix{dfõ
    m%n,;ajh fyda ;Sj%;djh ;SrKh lrk w;r idudkHfhka iu;=,s; ia:dkfha isg WÉph olajd ukskq
    ,efí' úoHq;a ix{d j,§ th fndfyda úg fjda,aÜ j,ska m%ldY jk w;r Yío ;rx. j,§ th fvisn,a
    fyda mSvk tAll j,ska ukskq ,efí'
    Amplitude is a fundamental property of a signal because it defines its magnitude, influencing power and
    transmission quality. / úia;drh hkq ix{djl uQ,sl .=Kdx.hla jkafka th tys úYd,;ajh" n,h iy
    iïfm%aIK .=Khg n,mEï lrk neúks'
    Frequency / ixLHd;h
    Frequency refers to the number of cycles a signal completes per unit of time, measured in Hertz (Hz). It
    determines the rate at which the signal oscillates and is crucial for understanding signal behavior over
    time. Frequency is a fundamental property of a signal, as it defines how fast the signal repeats itself, which
    is critical for applications like communication and sound engineering.
    ixLHd;h hkq ix{djla tAll ld,hl§ iïmQ¾K lrk pl% .Kkhs' th y¾Üia ^Hz& tAllfhka ukskq
    ,nk w;r" ix{dj foda,kh jk fõ.h ;SrKh lrhs' ld,h;a iuÕ ix{dfõ yeisÍu f;areï .ekSug
    th b;du;a jeo.;a fõ' ixLHd;h hkq ix{djl uQ,sl .=Kdx.hls" ukao th ix{djla fld;rï
    fõ.fhka mqkrdj¾;kh fõo hkak ks¾jpkh lrhs' fuh ikaksfõok yd Yío bxðfkare úoHdj jeks
    lafIa;%j,§ b;du;a jeo.;a fõ'
    Phase / l,dj
    Phase describes the position of a waveform relative to a reference point in its cycle, often measured in
    degrees or radians. It is essential for analyzing the timing relationship between signals. Phase is a key
    property of a signal, as it helps in understanding the alignment or synchronization of signals, which is vital
    in systems like wireless communication and audio processing.
    l,dj hkq ;rx. rEmhla tys pl%hl§ fhduq ,laIHhlg idfmalaIj we;s ia:dkhhs' th wxYl fyda
    f¾ähka j,ska ukskq ,nk w;r" ix{d w;r ld,Sk iïnkaO;d úYaf,aIKh lsÍug w;HjYH fõ' l,dj
    hkq ix{djl uQ,sl .=Kdx.hls" ukao th ix{d iïmd;kh fyda iïnkaO;djh f;areï .ekSug WmldÍ
    fõ' th f¾äfhda ikaksfõok yd Y%jH ieliqï jeks lafIa;%j,§ b;du;a jeo.;afõ'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                -89-                                           #ictfromabc



    Wavelength / ;rx. wdhduh
    Wavelength is the distance between two consecutive points in the same phase of a signal, such as peak to
    peak. It is inversely related to frequency and is significant in wave propagation studies. Wavelength is a
    critical property of a signal, especially in wave-based systems like radio and light, as it determines how
    the signal propagates through space.
    ;rx. wdhduh hkq ix{djl tlu wjêfha mj;sk ,laIH folla w;r ÿrhs ^Wod( WÉpfha isg WÉph
    olajd&' th ixLHd;hg m%;sf,dauj iïnkaO jk w;r" ;rx. m%pdrK wOHhkj,§ jeo.;a fõ' ;rx.
    wdhduh hkq ix{djl uQ,sl .=Kdx.hls" úfYaIfhka f¾äfhda yd wdf,dal moaO;s jeks ;rx.-wdOdß;
    moaO;sj,§" ukao th ix{djla wjldYh yryd m%pdrKh jk wdldrh ;SrKh lrhs'

    Time / ld,h
    Time represents the duration over which a signal is observed or analyzed. It is a fundamental parameter
    for understanding signal evolution and is often plotted on the x-axis in signal graphs. Time is a fundamental
    property of a signal, as it provides the context for how the signal changes or evolves, making it essential
    for time-domain analysis.
    ld,h hkq ix{djla ksÍlaIKh fyda úYaf,aIKh lrk ld, iSudjhs' ix{dfõ fjkiaùï fyda mßKduh
    f;areï .ekSug th uQ,sl mrdñ;shla jk w;r" fndfyda úg ix{d m%ia;drj, x wlaIfha olajkq ,efí'

    ms<sn| ikao¾Nh imhhs' ld,-wxY (time-domain) úYaf,aIKh i|yd th w;HjYH fõ'

    Impulse / wdfõ.h
    An impulse is a sudden, short-lived spike in a signal, often modeled as a Dirac delta function. It represents
    a brief burst of energy and is used to analyze system responses to abrupt changes. Impulse is a specific
    signal property used to study system behavior in response to sudden inputs, making it relevant in signal
    processing and system analysis. While not a core property like amplitude or frequency, it is important for
    understanding transient signal characteristics.
    wdfõ.hla hkq ix{djl yÈis flá ld,Sk iamhsla" fndfyda úg ä/la fv,agd Y%s;hla f,i wdo¾YKh

    lsÍug Ndú;d lrhs' wdfõ.h hkq yÈis fhojqï j,g m%;spdr jYfhka moaO;s yeisÍï wOHhkh lsÍug

    jeo.;a fõ'

     Guided Media / kshuq udOHh
     Guided media refers to communication channels where signals are transmitted through physical pathways,
     such as cables or wires. Examples include twisted-pair cables, coaxial cables, and optical fibers. The signal
     is confined within the medium, ensuring controlled and reliable transmission.
     kshuq udOHh hkq flan,a fyda jh¾ jeks fN!;sl ud¾. yryd ix{d iïfm%aIKh jk ikaksfõok kd,sld

    Unguided media involves the transmission of signals through wireless means, without the use of physical

    Infrared / wfOdarla; lsrK
    Infrared waves fall just below visible light in the electromagnetic spectrum, with frequencies typically

    Used in short-range communication, such as remote controls, wireless keyboards, and infrared sensors.

    Ndú;d lrhs'
    Infrared communication is limited to short distances and typically requires line-of-sight. It does not

 5. The frequency modulation technique is used to change only
    ixLHd; uQ¾ck (frequency modulation) Ys,am l%uh Ndú;fhka fjkia lrkq ,nkafka"
    1)   the amplitude and frequency / úia;drh iy ixLHd;h muKs'
    2)   the amplitude, frequency and phase / úia;drh" ixLHd;h iy l,dj muKs'

    4)   the frequency / ixLHd;h muKs'

    Answer : 4)
    Modulation - uqyq¾ckh
    Modulation is defined as the process of modifying a carrier signal by varying its properties (amplitude,
    frequency, or phase) to encode information. It is essential for efficient signal transmission, especially in
    communication systems. / uqyq¾ckh hkq jdyl ix{djl .=Kdx. ^úia;drh" ixLHd;h" l,dj& fjkia
    lsÍfuka f;dr;=re fla;kh lsÍu i|yd fjkia lsÍfï l%shdj,sh f,i w¾: oelafõ' úfYaIfhka
    ikaksfõok moaO;sj," ld¾hlaIu ix{d iïfm%aIKh i|yd th w;HjYH fõ'

    Amplitude Modulation (AM) - úia;drh uqyq¾ckh
    In AM, the amplitude of the carrier signal is varied in accordance with the information signal,
    while frequency and phase remain constant. This means only the amplitude changes, and the other
    properties (frequency and phase) stay unchanged.
    AM ys" jdyl ix{dfõ úia;drh f;dr;=re ix{djg wkql+,j fjkia jk w;r ixLHd;h iy l,dj
    ksh;j mj;S' fuhska woyia lrkafka úia;drh muKla fjkia jk w;r wfkl=;a .=Kdx. ^ixLHd;h
    iy l,dj& fkdfjkiaj mj;S'

    Connection to the options: AM involves changes to (1) the amplitude and frequency is incorrect because
    frequency is not altered. The correct description aligns with changes to amplitude only, but since
    "amplitude only" is not an option, the closest match is (3) the amplitude and phase, though phase is not
    actually changed.
    AM ys fjkialï we;=<;a fõ, (1) ixLHd;h fjkia fkdjk ksid úia;drh iy ixLHd;h jerÈhs' ksjerÈ
    úia;rh úia;drhg muKla fjkiaùï iuÕ fm<.iajhs" kuq;a úia;drh muKla úl,amhla fkdjk
    neúka" wdikak;u .e,mSu (3) úia;drh iy l,djhs" kuq;a l,dj we;a; jYfhkau fjkia fkdfõ'

    Frequency Modulation (FM) - ixLHd; uqyq¾ckh
    In FM, the frequency of the carrier signal is altered based on the information signal, while amplitude and
    phase are kept unchanged. Only the frequency is modified, and amplitude and phase remain constant.
    FM ys" jdyl ix{dfõ ixLHd;h f;dr;=re ix{dj u; mokïj fjkia lrkq ,nk w;r" úia;drh

    l,dj fjkia fkdjk neúka fuh ksjerÈ ms<s;=rhs'

    Phase Modulation (PM) - l,dj uqyq¾ckh
    In PM, the phase of the carrier signal is shifted in response to the information signal, while amplitude and
    frequency remain unaltered. Only the phase is changed, and amplitude and frequency stay the same.

 6. Consider the following statements:
    my; m%ldY i,lkak:
    A. A hub connects only the networked computers but a switch connects multiple devices.
       kdNsh (hub) u.ska cd,.; mß.Kl muKla iïnkaO flfrk w;r iaúph (switch) u.ska nyqúO

    2)   A, B and D only / A, B iy D muKs
    3)   A, C and D only / A, C iy D muKs

    Answer : 1)
    Statement A
    This statement is true / fuu m%ldYh i;H fõ'
    A hub is a basic networking device that connects computers in a network, but it lacks intelligence and
    broadcasts data to all connected devices. A switch, on the other hand, can connect various devices (like
    computers, printers, etc.) and intelligently forwards data only to the intended recipient.
    kdNsh hkq uQ,sl cd, Wmdx.hla jk w;r th mß.Kl cd,hlg iïnkaO lrhs" kuq;a th nqoaêu;a
    fkdjk w;r o;a; ish¨u iïnkaê; Wmdx.j,g m%pdrKh lrhs' switch tl" wfkla w;g" úúO Wmdx.
    ^mß.Kl" uqøK hka;%" wdÈh& iïnkaO l< yels w;r o;a; nqoaêu;a f,i woyia l< ,nkakdg muKla
    fhduq lrhs'

    Statement B
    This statement is true / fuu m%ldYh i;H fõ'
    A switch is more advanced and can manage individual ports, control traffic, and configure VLANs (Virtual
    Local Area Networks) for better network segmentation and security.
    iaúÉh jvd;a ÈhqKq Wmdx.hla jk w;r th ;ks flfjks l<ukdlrKh lsÍu" .ukd.ukh md,kh
    lsÍu iy ^VLAN – Virtual Local Area Networks) ieliSu u.ska cd, fldgialrKh iy wdrlaIdj jeä
    ÈhqKq lrhs'

    Statement C
    This statement is true / fuu m%ldYh i;H fõ'
    A hub operates at the Physical Layer (Layer 1) and transmits data in the form of raw bits. A switch operates
    at the Data Link Layer (Layer 2) and uses frames (for Layer 2) and packets (for Layer 3) to transmit data
    more efficiently.
    kdNsh fN!;sl ia:rfha (Layer 1) l%shd lrk w;r o;a; wuq ìgq (bits) f,i iïfm%aYkh lrhs' iaúph

    Statement D
    This statement is false / fuu m%ldYh wi;H fõ'

    What is a Server?
    fiajdodhlhla hkq l=ulao@

    A server is a powerful computer or software that provides services, data, or resources to other computers
    (clients) over a network. Servers can handle multiple requests simultaneously and are essential for the
    functioning of the internet and internal networks.
    fiajdodhlhla hkq cd,hla yryd fjk;a mß.Kl ^fiajd,dNSka& fj; fiajd" o;a; fyda iïm;a imhk
    n,j;a mß.Klhla fyda uDÿldx.hls' fiajdodhlhkag tljr b,a,Sï lsysmhla yeisrúh yels w;r
    wka;¾cd,fha iy wNHka;r cd,j, l%shdldß;ajh i|yd w;HjYH fõ'

    Types of Servers & Their Functions
    fiajdodhl j¾. iy tAjdfha ld¾hhka

    Proxy Server – Acts as a middleman between users and the internet, improving security and performance
    mßYS,lhka iy wka;¾cd,h w;r w;rueÈfhl= f,i l%shd lrhs" wdrlaIdj iy ld¾h idOkh jeä
    ÈhqKq lrhs'

    Mail Server – Handles email sending, receiving, and storage (e.g., Gmail, Outlook).
    Bfï,a heùu" ,eîu iy .nvd lsÍu yiqrejhs'

    DNS Server – Converts domain names into IP addresses for internet browsing
    wka;¾cd, .fõIKh i|yd jiï kdu IP ,smsk njg mßj¾;kh lrhs'
    (e.g., Google DNS - 8.8.8.8).

    Web Server – Hosts websites and serves web pages (e.g., Apache, Nginx).
    fjí wvú i;aldrl;ajh imhk w;r fjí msgq fiajd imhhs'

    File Server – Stores and shares files across a network (e.g., Windows File Server).
    cd,hla yryd f.dkq .nvd lr fnod .kS'

    Database Server – Manages and provides access to databases (e.g., MySQL, PostgreSQL).
    o;a; iuqodhka l<ukdlrKh lr m%fõYh imhhs'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -97-                                           #ictfromabc



    Application Server – Runs applications for users over a network (e.g., Tomcat, WebSphere).
    cd,hla yryd mßYS,lhska i|yd fhÿï Odjkh lrhs'

   1. Which of the following statements regarding guided and unguided media are correct?
      kshuq (guided) udOH iy kshuq fkdjk (unguided) udOH iïnkaOfhka my; ljr j.ka;s i;H fõo?
          A. Guided media transmission supports higher data speeds than unguided media transmission.

          1) A,B and C only. 2) A,B and D only. 3) A,C and D only. 4) B,C and D only. 5) All of the above.

          Guided media / kshuq udOH
          Guided media refers to the transmission of data over a physical path or medium. This type of media
          provides a clear, defined route for the signal to travel from the sender to the receiver. Guided media is
          essential in wired communication systems, where the physical connection ensures that the data is
          transmitted efficiently and with minimal interference.
          kshuq udOH hkq fN!;sl ud¾.hla fyda udOHhla yryd o;a; iïfm%aIKh lsÍuhs' fuu j¾.fha udOH
          uÕska ix{dj hjkakdf.a isg .%dylhd olajd .uka lsÍu i|yd meyeÈ,s" ksYaÑ; ud¾.hla imhhs'
          /yeka.; ikaksfõok moaO;sj, ud¾f.damfoaYl udOH w;HjYH jk w;r" fN!;sl iïnkaO;djh o;a;
          ld¾hlaIuj iy wju ueÈy;aùï iys;j iïfm%aIKh jk nj iy;sl lrhs'

          Types of Guided Media / kshuq udOH j¾.

          1) Twisted-Pair Cable / weUreKq hq., /yeka
                Consists of pairs of insulated copper wires twisted together.
                tlg weUreKq mßjrKh lrk ,o ;U /yeka hq., j,ska iukaú; fõ'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                              -99-                                          #ictfromabc


       Commonly used in telephone networks and local area networks (LANs).

    Non Guided Media / kshuq fkdjk udOH
    Non-guided media, also known as unguided media or wireless media, refers to the method of transmitting
    data without using a physical conductor. Instead of traveling through cables or optical fibers, signals in
    non-guided media are broadcast through the air, vacuum, or even water. This type of transmission relies
    on electromagnetic waves, such as radio waves, microwaves, or infrared signals, to carry data from one
    point to another.

 2. What is the process carried-out in the modulation technique in data transmission?

       wju úlD;shla (distortion) iys;j f;dr;=re yqjudre lsÍu

    Answer : 1 )

    Modulation / uQ¾ckh
    Modulation is a technique in data transmission where information (like digital data or audio signals) is
    encoded onto a carrier signal to enable it to travel effectively over a transmission medium. This process is
    essential for both analog and digital communication systems, allowing data to be transmitted over long
    distances with minimal loss or distortion.
    uQ¾ckh hkq o;a; iïfm%aIKfha ;dlaIKhls" tys§ f;dr;=re ^äðg,a o;a; fyda Y%jH ix{d jeks&
    iïfm%aIK udOHhla yryd M,odhs f,i .uka lsÍug yels jk mßÈ jdyl ix{djla u; fla;kh
    flfra' fuu l%shdj,sh m%;siu iy ixLHdxl ikaksfõok moaO;s folgu w;HjYH jk w;r" wju
    w,dNhla fyda úlD;shla iys;j È.= ÿrlg o;a; iïfm%aIKh lsÍug bv i,ihs'

    Types of Modulation / uQ¾ck j¾.
    There are different ways to modulate signal, each manipulating a specific characteristic of the carrier signal
    ix{djla uQ¾ckh lsÍug úúO l%u we;" tA iEu tllau jdyl ix{dfõ ksYaÑ; ,laIKhla yiqrejhs

    Amplitude Modulation / úia;dr uQ¾ckh (AM)
    Changes the amplitude (strength) of the carrier signal to represent the data.
    o;a; ksrEmKh lsÍu i|yd jdyl ix{dfõ úia;drh ^Yla;sh& fjkia lrhs'
    Used in AM radio and some older analog TV broadcasts.
    AM .=jka úÿ,sfha iy iuyr merKs wekf,d.a rEmjdyskS úldYkj, Ndú;d fõ'

    Frequency Modulation / ixLHd; uQ¾ckh (FM)
    Adjusts the frequency (number of oscillations per second) of the carrier signal according to the data.
    o;a; wkqj jdyl ix{dfõ ixLHd;h ^;;amrhg foda,kh jk ixLHdj& ilihs'
    Commonly used in FM radio broadcasting, where it provides better sound quality and noise resistance
    than AM.
    FM fraäfhda úldYkfha§ nyq,j Ndú;d jk w;r" th AM j,g jvd fyd| Yío ;;a;aj iy Yío
    m%;sfrdaOhla imhhs'

    Phase Modulation / l,d uQ¾ckh (PM)
    Alters the phase (the position of the wave) of the carrier signal to carry information.
    f;dr;=re /f.k hdu i|yd jdyl ix{dfõ wÈhr ^;rx.fha msysàu& fjkia lrhs'
    Often used in digital communication systems and is the basis for some advanced modulation techniques.
    fndfyda úg äðg,a ikaksfõok moaO;sj, Ndú;d jk w;r iuyr Wiia uQ¾ck Ys,amSh l%u i|yd
    moku fõ'

     Digital Modulation Techniques / äðg,a uQ¾ck ;dlaIKsl l%u
     Digital modulation is used to transmit binary data (0s and 1s) over a carrier signal, typically over long
     distances or through radio waves.
     idudkHfhka È.= ÿrla fyda fraäfhda ;rx. yryd jdyl ix{djla yryd oaúuh o;a; ^0 iy 1&
     iïfm%aIKh lsÍug äðg,a uQ¾ckh Ndú;d lrhs'

     Amplitude Shift Keying / úia;dr iSre udrej (ASK)
     Changes the amplitude of the carrier signal to represent binary data.
     oaúuh o;a; ksrEmKh lsÍug jdyl ix{dfõ úia;drh fjkia lrhs'

     Frequency Shift Keying / ixLHd; iSre udrej (FSK)
     Changes the frequency of the carrier signal based on the binary data being sent.
     hjk oaúuh o;a; u; mokïj jdyl ix{dfõ ixLHd;h fjkia lrhs'

     Phase Shift Keying / l,d iSre udrej (PSK)
     Alters the phase of the carrier signal to represent data. This is commonly used in digital communication
     systems, such as Wi-Fi and cellular networks.
     o;a; ksrEmKh lsÍu i|yd jdyl ix{dfõ wÈhr fjkia lrhs' fuh Wi-Fi iy ffi,Sh cd, jeks
     äðg,a ikaksfõok moaO;sj, nyq,j Ndú;d fõ'

     Why Modulation is Needed / uQ¾ckh wjYH jkafka wehs

     Efficient Transmission Over Long Distances / È.= ÿr yryd ld¾hlaIu iïfm%aIKh
     High-frequency signals can travel longer distances without significant loss, which is why modulation is
     essential for transmitting data effectively over various media.
     wê-ixLHd; ix{dj,g ie,lsh hq;= w,dNhlska f;drj jeä ÿrla .uka l< yels w;r" úúO udOH
     yryd M,odhS f,i o;a; iïfm%aIKh lsÍu i|yd fudähqf,aIka w;HjYH jkafka tneúks'

     Multi-Channel Communication / nyq kd,sld ikaksfõokh
     Different data streams can be transmitted on different carrier frequencies, allowing multiple signals to be
     sent simultaneously on same medium (e.g., different radio stations broadcasting on different frequencies).
     úúO o;a; m%jdyhka úúO jdyl ixLHd; u; iïfm%aIKh l< yels w;r" tlu udOHhla u; tljr
     ix{d lsysmhla heùug bv i,ihs ^Wod( úúO ixLHd; u; úldYkh lrk úúO .=jka úÿ,s uOHia:dk&'

     Bandwidth Efficiency / l,dm m<, ld¾hlaIu;dj
     Modulation techniques make better use of the available bandwidth, allowing for more data to be
     transmitted within the same frequency range.
     uQ¾ck Ys,amSh l%u mj;sk l,dm m<, jvd fyd¢ka Ndú;d lrhs" tu ixLHd; mrdih ;=< jeä o;a;
     iïfm%aIKh lsÍug bv i,ihs'

     Noise Resistance / Yío m%;sfrdaOh
     Some modulation techniques, like FM and certain digital modulation schemes, are more resistant to noise
     and interference, ensuring clearer signals and more accurate data transfer.
     FM iy we;eï äðg,a uQ¾ck fhdackd l%u jeks iuyr uQ¾ck l%u" Yíohg iy ueÈy;aùïj,g jvd
     m%;sfrdaë jk w;r" meyeÈ,s ix{d iy jvd;a ksjerÈ o;a; yqjudrej iy;sl lrhs'

     The process of modulation involves encoding information onto a carrier signal to prepare it for
     transmission over a medium (like air, cable, or fiber). This allows data to travel more effectively and
     efficiently over long distances or shared channels.
     uQ¾ck l%shdj,shg udOHhla ^jd;h" flan,a fyda ;ka;= jeks& yryd iïfm%aIKh lsÍu i|yd f;dr;=re
     jdyl ix{djlg fla;kh lsÍu we;=<;a fõ' fuu.ska o;a; È.= ÿrla fyda fnod.;a kd,sld yryd
     jvd;a M,odhSj iy ld¾hlaIuj .uka lsÍug bv i,ihs'

     Steps in the Modulation Process / uQ¾ck l%shdj,sfha mshjr

     Choosing a Carrier Signal / jdyl ix{djla f;dard .ekSu
     A high-frequency signal, known as the carrier signal, is selected. This signal will "carry" the data across
     the transmission medium. The high frequency of the carrier enables it to travel longer distances and
     reduces susceptibility to interference.
     jdyl ix{dj f,i ye¢kafjk wê-ixLHd; ix{djla f;dard .kq ,efí' fuu ix{dj iïfm%aIK udOHh
     yryd o;a; /f.k hhs' jdylfha by< ixLHd;h th È.= ÿrla .uka lsÍug yelshdj ,nd fok w;r
     ndOd lsÍïj,g f.dÿre ùfï yelshdj wvq lrhs'

    Encoding Information (Modulating Signal) / fla;k f;dr;=re ^ix{d fudähqf,aÜ&
    The information or data that needs to be transmitted (known as the modulating signal) is encoded onto the
    carrier signal by modifying one of the carrier’s characteristics.
    iïfm%aIKh lsÍug wjYH f;dr;=re fyda o;a; ^fudähqf,aáka is.ak,a f,i ye¢kafõ& jdylhdf.a tla
    ,laIKhla fjkia lsÍfuka jdyl ix{djg fla;kh flfra'

    Creating the Modulated Signal / uQ¾ck ix{dj ks¾udKh lsÍu
    The carrier signal and the modulating signal are combined, producing the modulated signal. This signal
    now represents the original information and is ready for transmission.
    jdyl ix{dj iy fudähqf,aáka ix{dj tAldnoaO ù" fudähqf,agâ ix{dj ksmojhs' fuu ix{dj oeka
    uq,a f;dr;=re ksfhdackh lrk w;r iïfm%aIKh i|yd iQodkï fõ'

    Transmitting the Signal / ix{d iïfma%IKh lsÍu

    with minimal data loss.
    uQ¾ck ix{dj f;dard.;a iïfm%aIK udOHh yryd hjkq ,nk w;r" wju o;a; w,dNhla iys;j jeä
    ÿrla .uka l< yel'

    Demodulation at the Receiver / .%dylfha úuQ¾ck
    At the receiver end, the process is reversed. Demodulation extracts the original information from the
    modulated signal, recovering the original data for interpretation and use.

    ksjerÈ ms<s;=r 1) fõ'

    In the modulation process, information (data) is encoded onto a carrier signal to prepare it for transmission.
    This involves modifying certain characteristics of the carrier signal (such as amplitude, frequency, or
    phase) to represent the data, allowing it to travel efficiently over the chosen medium. This is essential in
    both analog and digital communication.

 3. Which of the following statements about bus topology are incorrect?
    nia iaM,lh (bus topology) ms<sn| my; ljr j.ka;s idjoH fõ o?
    A. Computers and network devices are connected to a single cable.

       ish¨u .ukd.uk (traffic) olaIsKdj¾;j fyda jdudj¾;j fyda isÿ fõ'
    C. Bandwidth is shared among the nodes.

       iEu fkdavqjlau ;ukaf.a wi,ajeishka fofokdg iïnkaO lr we;'

    1) A and B only.    2) A and D only.     3) B and C only.     4) B and D only.    5) C and D only.
       A yd B muKs.        A yd D muKs.         B yd C muKs.         B yd D muKs.        C yd D muKs.

    Answer : 4 )

    Bandwidth / l,dm m<,
    Bandwidth in networking and data transmission refers to the maximum amount of data that can be
    transmitted over a network or internet connection within a specific period, usually measured in bits per
    second (bps), such as Mbps (Megabits per second) or Gbps (Gigabits per second). Bandwidth determines
    how much information can flow from one point to another on a network in a given amount of time.
    cd,lrKfha iy o;a; iïfm%aIKfha l,dm m<, hkq idudkHfhka ;;amrhg ìgq ^bps& Mbps ^;;amrhg
    fu.dìÜ& fyda Gbps ^;;amrhlg .s.dìÜ& j,ska ukskq ,nk" ksYaÑ; ld, iSudjla ;=< cd,hla fyda

    Nodes / fkdavq
    In computer networking, a node refers to any physical or virtual device that is connected to a network and
    capable of sending, receiving, or forwarding data. Nodes are essential components in a network, allowing
    data to flow between devices and systems.

    Bus topology is a network layout where all devices are connected to a single main cable, often called the

    receives the same signal simultaneously.

    Star Topology / ;re ia:,lh

    Star topology is a networking layout where all devices are
    connected to a central device, typically a switch or hub. This
    central device acts like a traffic controller, receiving data from one
    device and then forwarding it to the intended recipient on the
    network.

    Ring Topology / uqÿ ia:,lh
    In a ring topology, devices are connected in a closed loop, forming a
    circular path for data transmission. Each device acts as a repeater,
    receiving data packets, regenerating the signal to maintain its strength,
    and then forwarding it to the next device in the ring.

    Mesh topology / oe,a ia:,lh
    Mesh topology is a web-like network structure where devices are
    interconnected with each other, as opposed to relying on a central hub
    or switch like in star topology. Imagine a fishnet where each knot
    connects to multiple other knots, creating a redundant network of
    pathways. In a mesh network, data can travel along multiple paths
    between devices, providing flexibility and fault tolerance.

    iaúphla u; hemSug m%;súreoaOj Wmdx. tlsfkl yd iïnkaO jk

 4. Consider the following statement with a blank.

    Media Access Control / udOH m%fõY md,kh (MAC)
    Media Access Control (MAC) refers to a unique identifier assigned to the network interface of devices on
    a local network. This identifier, called the MAC address, is essential for managing and controlling access
    to the network, allowing devices to communicate with each other on the same local network.
    udOH m%fõY md,kh (MAC) hkq foaYSh cd,hl Wmdx.j, cd, w;=reuqyqK;g mjrd we;s wkkH
    y÷kd.ekSuls' MAC ,smskh f,i ye¢kafjk fuu y÷kd.ekSu" cd,h fj; m%fõYh l<ukdlrKh
    lsÍu iy md,kh lsÍu i|yd w;HjYH jk w;r" tlu m%dfoaYSh cd,hl Wmdx. tlsfkld iuÕ
    ikaksfõokh lsÍug bv i,ihs'

    Key Characteristics of MAC / MAC ys m%Odk ,laIK

    •   Unique Identifier / wkkH y÷kd.ekSu
        Each MAC address is unique to a device’s network interface card (NIC). This uniqueness allows each
        device on a network to be individually identified, even within the same network segment.
        iEu MAC ,smskhlau Wmdx.fha cd, w;=reuqyqK;a ldâm;g ^NIC& wkkH fõ' fuu iqúfYaI;ajh
        cd,hl we;s iEu Wmdx.hlau tlu cd, fldgi ;=< jqjo ;ks ;ksj y÷kd .ekSug bv i,ihs'

    •   Format /wdlD;sh
        A MAC address is a 48-bit number, typically represented in hexadecimal notation (e.g.,
        00:1A:2B:3C:4D:5E). The address is divided into six pairs of hexadecimal numbers, separated by
        colons or hyphens. / MAC ,smskhla hkq 48-bit wxlhls" idudkHfhka IâoYuh wxlkhlska ksrEmKh
        flf¾ ^Wod( 00:1A:2B:3C:4D:5E& ,smskh IâoYu ixLHd hq., yhlg fnod we;" tAjd fld,ka
        fyda yhs*ka j,ska fjka lr we;'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                -110-                                        #ictfromabc


    •   Layer 2 of the OSI Model /OSI wdlD;sfha 2 jk ia:rh
        MAC addresses operate at the Data Link layer (Layer 2) of the OSI model. This layer is responsible
        for node-to-node data transfer and error checking in direct data links.
        MAC ,smsk OSI wdlD;sfha o;a; ikaOdk ia:rfha ^Layer 2& l%shd;aul fõ' fuu ia;rh node-to-
        node o;a; yqjudrej iy iDcq o;a; ine¢ j, fodaI mÍlaId lsÍu i|yd j.lsj hq;=h'

    Two Parts of the MAC Address / MAC ,smskfha fldgia folla

    •   Organizationally Unique Identifier / ixúOdkd;aulj wkkH y÷kd.ekSï (OUI)
        The first 24 bits (or three hexadecimal pairs) represent the manufacturer or vendor of the network
        interface card. / m<uq ìgq 24 ^fyda Iâ oYu hq., ;=kla& cd, w;=reuqyqK;a ldâmf;a ksIamdolhd
        fyda fjf<kaod ksfhdackh lrhs'

    •   Device Identifier / Wmdx. y÷kd.ekSu
        The last 24 bits (or three hexadecimal pairs) are specific to the device itself
        wjidk ìgq 24 ^fyda Iâ oYu hq., ;=kla& Wmdx.hgu úfYaIs; fõ'

    Uses of MAC Addresses / MAC ,smsk Ndú;h

    •   Local Network Communication / foaYSh cd, ikaksfõokh
        Within a local network, data packets are sent to devices using their MAC addresses, enabling direct
        communication between devices on the same LAN.
        foaYSh cd,hla ;=<" o;a; melÜ Tjqkaf.a MAC ,smsk Ndú;fhka Wmdx. fj; hjkq ,efí" tlu
        LAN u; Wmdx. w;r iDcq ikaksfõokh il%Sh lrhs'

    •   Access Control / m%fõY md,kh
        MAC addresses can be used to control access to a network, such as in MAC address filtering, where
        only approved devices are allowed to connect.
        wkqu; Wmdx.j,g muKla iïnkaO ùug wjir § we;s MAC ,smsk fmryk jeks cd,hlg
        m%fõYh md,kh lsÍug MAC ,smsk Ndú;d l< yel'

    •   Network Troubleshooting /cd, fodaY ksrdlrKh
        Network administrators use MAC addresses to track and troubleshoot devices on the network, helping
        to identify devices with specific network issues.
        cd, mßmd,lhska cd,fha Wmdx. ksÍlaIKh lsÍug iy fodaY ksrdlrKh lsÍug MAC ,smsk Ndú;d
        lrhs" úfYaIs; cd, .eg¿ iys; Wmdx. y÷kd .ekSug WmldÍ fõ'

    MAC Address / MAC ,smskh
    Unique to a device’s physical hardware and used within a local network for identification.
    Wmdx.fha fN!;sl oDVdx. i|yd wkkH jk w;r y÷kd.ekSu i|yd foaYSh cd,hla ;=< Ndú;d fõ'

    IP Address / IP ,smskh
    A logical address assigned to a device for communication across different networks, typically over the
    internet. / idudkHfhka wka;¾cd,h yryd úúO cd, yryd ikaksfõokh i|yd Wmdx.hlg mjrd we;s
    ;d¾lsl ,smskhls'

    The correct answer is, 3) hexadecimal
    ksjerÈ ms<s;=r kï" 3) Iâ oYu

    A Media Access Control (MAC) address is typically represented in hexadecimal numbers. A MAC address
    is a unique identifier assigned to network interfaces for communication on a network, and it is commonly
    written as six pairs of hexadecimal digits (e.g., A1:B2:C3:D4:E5:F6). Each pair of hexadecimal digits
    represents 8 bits, or one byte, of information, making it ideal for representing 48-bit MAC address format.
    udOH m%fõY md,k (MAC) ,smskhla idudkHfhka Iâ oYu ixLHd j,ska ksrEmKh flf¾' MAC ,smskhla
    hkq cd,hl ikaksfõokh i|yd cd, w;=reuqyqK;a i|yd mjrd we;s wkkH y÷kd.ekSula jk w;r
    th idudkHfhka ,shd we;af;a IâoYu b,lalï hq., yhla f,ih ^Wod( A1:B2:C3:D4:E5:F6& iEu
    Iâ oYuh b,lalï hq.,hlau ìgq 8la fyda tla nhsghla f;dr;=re ksfhdackh lrhs" th ìÜ 48 MAC
    ,smsk wdlD;sh ksfhdackh lsÍu i|yd jvd;a iqÿiq fõ'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                             -111-                                         #ictfromabc


    Why the Other Options Are Incorrect,
    fjk;a úl,am jerÈ wehs"

    1) Binary / oaúuh
       Although the underlying data is stored in binary, MAC addresses are not represented in binary format
       for human readability. / mdol o;a; oaúuh jYfhka .nvd lr we;;a" MAC ,smsk ñksia lshùfï
       yelshdj i|yd oaúuh wdlD;sfhka ksfhdackh fkdfõ'
    2) Decimal / oYu
       MAC addresses are not written in decimal format because decimal representation would make them
       harder to read and manage.
       MAC ,smsk oYu wdlD;sfhka ,shd ke;"ukao oYu ksrEmKh uÕska tAjd lshùug iy l<ukdlrKh
       lsÍug wmyiq jkq we;'
    4) Natural / iajNdúl
       Natural numbers do not apply to the formatting of MAC addresses.
       MAC ,smsk yev;, .ekaùug iajNdúl wxl wod< fkdfõ'
    5) Octal / wIagl
       MAC addresses are not represented in octal. hexadecimal is the preferred format for clarity and ease
       of reading. / MAC ,smsk wIagl j,ska ksfhdackh fkdfõ' Iâ oYu hkq meyeÈ,slu iy lshùfï
       myiqj i|yd jvd;a leu;s wdlD;shhs'

    Therefore, hexadecimal is the correct and standard format for MAC address representation.
    tneúka" Iâ oYu hkq MAC ,smsk ksrEmKh i|yd ksjerÈ iy iïu; wdlD;shhs'

          i|yd 2 wvq lsÍu&'

          Digital Signal / wxls; ix{dj
          A digital signal is a type of signal that represents data or information using discrete levels, typically binary
          values like 0 and 1. These signals are fundamental to modern digital electronics, including computers,
          smartphones, and digital communication systems. Digital signals are contrasted with analog signals, which
          represent information using continuous ranges of values.
          wxls; ix{djla hkq úúla; uÜgï Ndú;d lrñka o;a; fyda f;dr;=re ksfhdackh lrk ix{d j¾.hls"

          Square Wave / fldgq ;rx.h
          A square wave is a type of periodic waveform that alternates between two distinct levels, typically high
          and low, with a fixed frequency and duty cycle. It is called a square wave because of its characteristic
          shape, which consists of sharp transitions between the high and low levels, resulting in a waveform that
          looks like a series of squares. / fldgq ;rx.hla hkq ia:djr ixLHd; iy rdcldß pl%hla iuÕ

    Key Points of MCQ

    (i) Guided media / kshuq udOH
        Guided media refers to the transmission of data over a physical path or medium. This type of media
        provides a clear, defined route for the signal to travel from the sender to the receiver. Guided media is
        essential in wired communication systems, where the physical connection ensures that the data is
        transmitted efficiently and with minimal interference.
        kshuq udOH hkq fN!;sl ud¾.hla fyda udOHhla yryd o;a; iïfm%aIKh lsÍuhs' fuu j¾.fha

    (ii) Non-Guided Media / kshuq fkdjk udOH
       Non-guided media, also known as unguided media or wireless media, refers to the method of

       fibers, signals in non-guided media are broadcast through the air, vacuum, or even water. This type of
       transmission relies on electromagnetic waves, such as radio waves, microwaves, or infrared signals,

    Key Points of MCQ

    (i) Analog Signal / m%;siu ix{dj
        An analog signal is a continuous signal that varies over time and represents physical quantities. Unlike
        digital signals, which take on discrete values, analog signals can have an infinite number of possible
        values within a given range. They are used to represent real-world phenomena like sound, light,
        temperature, and pressure, which naturally vary smoothly over time.
        m%;siu ix{djla hkq ld,h;a iuÕ fjkia jk iy fN!;sl m%udK ksfhdackh lrk wLKav

    (ii) Digital Signal / wxls; ix{dj
        A digital signal is a type of signal that represents data as a series of discrete values, typically in binary
        form (0s and 1s). Unlike analog signals, which are continuous and can take on an infinite number of
        values, digital signals are quantized and have only specific, discrete levels. This makes digital signals
        more robust against noise and interference, making them the preferred choice in most modern
        electronic and communication systems.

    Conversion of analog waves to digital waves / m%;siu ;rx. wxls; ;rx. njg mßj¾:kh

    enabling analog signals (such as sound, light, or other physical phenomena) to be represented, stored, and
    processed by digital systems. This process is typically achieved through Analog-to-Digital Conversion
    (ADC), which involves three main steps: sampling, quantization, and encoding.

    (i) Attenuation / jeyeßu
        Attenuation is the reduction in signal strength as it travels through a medium. It is a critical factor in

        networks, wireless systems, or audio transmission. Techniques like amplification, repeaters, and high-

    Pulse Code Modulation (PCM)
    iamkaok fla; uQ¾ckh
    Pulse Code Modulation (PCM) is a technique for converting analog signals into digital form by sampling
    the signal, quantizing the sampled values, and encoding them as binary numbers. It is widely used in
    telecommunications, audio, and video applications due to its ability to accurately represent analog signals
    in a digital format, enabling high-fidelity transmission and processing in digital systems.

    Synchronization
    iuuqyq¾;lrKh

    operate in harmony and are properly aligned in time. It is critical in a wide range of applications, from
    data communication and network operations to multimedia playback and multithreading in computing.
    Effective synchronization ensures accuracy, consistency, and smooth operation across different systems,
    preventing errors and enhancing performance.

    communication and audio recording.

    (iii) MAC

        Interconnection) model. It plays a critical role in controlling how data packets are transmitted between
        devices on a local network (such as Ethernet). MAC addresses ensure that data packets are delivered
        to the correct device within a local network.

    (v) UDP (User Datagram Protocol)
        UDP (User Datagram Protocol) is one of the core protocols of the Internet Protocol (IP) suite,
        operating at the transport layer (Layer 4) of the OSI model. Unlike TCP, UDP is a connectionless and
        unreliable protocol.

    Which of the above are the characteristics of UDP?

    1) A, B and C only. 2) A, C and E only. 3) A, D and E only. 4) B, C and D only. 5) B, D and E only.

    Answer : 5 )

    Transmission Control Protocol / iïfm%aIK md,k kshudj,sh (TCP)
    Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) is a core protocol of the Internet Protocol (IP) suite, which operates
    at the transport layer (Layer 4) of the OSI model. TCP is responsible for providing reliable, ordered, and
    error-checked delivery of data between applications running on hosts on a network. It is widely used for
    internet applications such as web browsing, email, and file transfer.

    Characteristics of TCP / TCP ys ,laIK

    (i) Connection-Oriented Communication / iïnkaO;d keUqre ikaksfõokh
        TCP requires a connection to be established between the sender and receiver before data is transmitted.
        TCP o;a; iïfm%aIKh lsÍug fmr hjkakd iy .%dylhd w;r iïnkaO;djhla we;s lr .ekSu
        wjYH fõ'

    (ii) Reliable Data Transfer / úYajikSh o;a; yqjudrej
         TCP ensures that each packet sent is acknowledged by the receiver. If an acknowledgment is not
         received, the packet is retransmitted.
         hjk iEu melÜgqjlau .%dylhd úiska ms<sf.k we;s nj TCP iy;sl lrhs' ms<s.ekSula fkd,enqfka
         kï" melÜgqj kej; iïfm%aIKh fõ'
        Each byte of data is assigned a sequence number. This helps in ensuring that data is reassembled in
        the correct order at the receiver’s end.
        iEu nhsÜ o;a;hlgu wkql%ñl wxlhla mjrkq ,efí' .%dylhdf.a fl<jf¾ o;a; ksjerÈ
        wkqms<sfj,g kej; tl,ia lr we;s nj iy;sl lsÍug fuh WmldÍ fõ'
        If a packet is lost during transmission, TCP detects the loss and retransmits the missing packet.
        iïfm%aIKh lsÍfï§ melÜgqjla ke;s jqjfyd;a" TCP w,dNh y÷kdf.k ke;sjQ melÜgqj kej;
        iïfm%aIKh lrhs'

    (iii) Error Detection and Correction / fodaI y÷kd .ekSu iy ksjerÈ lsÍu
          TCP includes a checksum field in its header for error-checking. If a packet is corrupted during
          transmission, it is detected, and the packet can be retransmitted.
          TCP tys YS¾Ifha fodaI mÍlaId lsÍu i|yd fplaiï lafIa;%hla we;=<;a fõ' iïfm%aIKh lsÍfï§
          melÜgqjla ¥Is; ù we;akï" th wkdjrKh lr .kakd w;r" melÜgqj kej; iïfm%aIKh l< yel'
        TCP ensures that data is transmitted accurately and any errors or issues are corrected.
        TCP uÕska o;a; ksjerÈj iïfm%aIKh jk nj iy;sl lrk w;r lsishï fodaIhla fyda .eg¿
        ksjerÈ lr we;'

    (iv) Congestion Control / ;onoh md,kh

         reduce the rate of data transmission to prevent further packet loss.

    (v) Full Duplex Communication / mQ¾K oaú;aj ikaksfõokh

    User Datagram Protocol / mßYS,l o;a; mKsúv kshudj,sh (UDP)
    User Datagram Protocol (UDP) is a communication protocol that operates at the transport layer (Layer 4)
    of the OSI (Open Systems Interconnection) model. UDP is a connectionless protocol that is designed for
    speed and efficiency, sacrificing reliability for simplicity and low latency. It is widely used in applications
    where quick data transmission is more important than accuracy, such as live streaming, online gaming,
    and Voice over IP (VoIP).
    mßYS,l o;a; mKsúv kshudj,sh (UDP) hkq OSI udÈ,sfha m%jdyk ia:rfha (Layer 4) l%shd;aul jk
    ikaksfõok fm%dfgdaflda,hls' UDP hkq fõ.h iy ld¾hlaIu;dj i|yd ks¾udKh lr we;s iïnkaO;d
    rys; kshudj,shls" ir, nj iy wvq m%udoh i|yd úYajikSh;ajh lem lrhs' iÔù m%jdyh" ud¾..;
    l%Svd iy Voice over IP (VoIP) jeks ksrjoH;djhg jvd blauka o;a; iïfm%aIKh jeo.;a jk fhÿïj,
    th nyq,j Ndú;d fõ'

    Characteristics of UDP / UDP ys ,laIK
    (i) Connectionless Communication / iïnkaO;d rys; ikaksfõokh
        UDP does not establish a connection before transmitting data. Data is sent in a stream of independent
        packets called datagrams.
        o;a; iïfm%aIKh lsÍug fmr UDP iïnkaO;djhla ia:dms; fkdlrhs' o;a; hjkq ,nkafka fâgd.%Eï
        f,i ye¢kafjk iajdëk melÜ m%jdyhla ;=< h'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -126-                                          #ictfromabc



    (ii) Unreliable Data Transfer / úYajdi l< fkdyels o;a; yqjudrej
         UDP does not provide acknowledgments for received packets. This means the sender has no way of
         knowing if the data was successfully received.
         UDP ,enqKq melÜ i|yd ms<s.ekSï ,nd fkdfoa' fuhska woyia lrkafka o;a; id¾:lj ,enqfka
         oehs oek .ekSug hjkakdg l%uhla fkdue;s njhs'
        If a packet is lost or corrupted during transmission, UDP does not retransmit it. The application must
        handle any data loss if necessary.
        iïfm%aIKh w;r;=r melÜgqjla ke;s ù fyda úkdY ù we;akï" UDP th kej; iïfm%aIKh
        fkdlrhs' wjYH kï fhÿu ´kEu o;a; w,dNhla yeisrúh hq;=h'

    (iii) No Ordered Delivery / weKjqï l< fnodyeÍula fkdue;
          UDP does not guarantee that packets will arrive in the order they were sent. Packets may be received
          out of sequence, or some may not arrive at all.
          melÜ tjQ wkqms<sfj,g meñfKk njg UDP iy;sl fkdfõ' melÜ wkqms<sfj,ska msg; ,eìh
          yelsh" ke;fyd;a iuyrla lsisfia;a fkdmeñfKkq we;'
        Unlike TCP, UDP does not assign sequence numbers to packets, so there is no inherent mechanism
        for reordering them.

        ieliSug wdfõksl hdka;%Khla fkdue;'

    (iv) Low Overhead / wvq fmdÿ ld¾h
         UDP has minimal protocol overhead because it does not need to manage connections,
         acknowledgments, or retransmissions. This makes UDP faster and more efficient in scenarios where
         speed is critical.

         lrhs'

        UDP is well-suited for applications that involve broadcasting (sending data to all devices on a network)
        or multicasting (sending data to a group of devices). This makes it ideal for streaming media and other
        real-time applications.
